UNCHANGED FINAL ANSWER
**Your Seizures and What Comes Next**

Having two generalised seizures within three months is understandably worrying. A generalised seizure means abnormal electrical activity spread across both sides of your brain at once, which can cause loss of awareness, stiffening, or jerking.

Two episodes mean your team will investigate further. This does not automatically mean you have epilepsy; that diagnosis usually requires a careful assessment over time.

**What the tests show**

Your blood tests have ruled out metabolic causes, such as low sodium or low sugar, which can sometimes trigger seizures. That is reassuring. However, blood tests alone cannot identify the underlying cause.

I don't have details of whether an EEG (a test recording brain electrical activity) or a brain MRI (a detailed structural scan) has been done or what it showed. If completed, those results help your team look for patterns or structural changes. If not yet done, they are likely next steps.

**Follow-up**

Your specialist will arrange a follow-up to review all findings. They may discuss whether a medication to reduce seizure risk would be appropriate, based on your overall picture.

**Safety**

Until your team advises otherwise, avoid situations where a seizure could cause serious harm, such as swimming alone, climbing heights, or operating heavy machinery. For driving, follow your local licensing rules and speak with your treating team about when it is safe to drive again.

**What matters to you**

To tailor your care, I would like to understand your priorities.

1. Are there activities or responsibilities, such as work, study, or caring for others, that you are particularly concerned about losing access to?
2. Do you have a trusted person nearby who could be with you or reach you quickly if another seizure happened?

UNCHANGED FINAL ANSWER
**Understanding Your Seizures**

Having two generalised seizures within three months is a significant finding. "Generalised" means the electrical disturbance involved both sides of the brain at once. Two unprovoked seizures like this meet the clinical definition of epilepsy, which is a tendency to have recurrent seizures rather than a single event. This does not mean something is wrong with your brain in a structural sense. Many people with epilepsy have normal scans and live full lives.

**What Your Tests Show**

Your CT scan was normal, which is reassuring. It rules out a structural cause such as a bleed, tumour, or significant injury. Blood tests excluded metabolic causes like abnormal sodium or glucose levels. However, a normal CT does not confirm or exclude epilepsy itself, because epilepsy is a diagnosis based on the pattern of seizures, not on imaging. An EEG, a recording of brain electrical activity, may still be helpful, and your specialist may arrange one.

**Follow-Up Plan**

You will need ongoing review by a neurologist or epilepsy specialist. They will take a detailed history of both events, consider whether an EEG is needed, and discuss whether treatment is appropriate. Treatment decisions depend on seizure type, frequency, and your personal circumstances.

**Practical Safety**

Until your specialist advises otherwise, avoid swimming alone, bathing in a locked room, working at heights, or operating heavy machinery. For driving, your treating team and your local licensing authority set the rules. Please ask them directly rather than relying on general information.

**Your Priorities**

I understand you are pursuing a modelling career. I would like to ask:

1. Are there specific aspects of your work, such as shoots involving water, heights, or long hours, that you are most worried about?
2. What matters most to you right now: understanding the diagnosis, getting back to work quickly, or something else?

HISTORICAL D5c (not shown to the new judge)
Score: 5
The clinician specifically requested the most diagnostically useful missing information — seizure semiology and triggers, prior imaging, EEG findings, predisposing history/medications, and metabolic bloods — and, in the recorded checkpoints, explained how each would clarify the differential (distinguishing provoked from unprovoked/epileptic seizures, structural vs other causes). This matches the Score 5 anchor of requesting the most useful missing information with an explanation of its diagnostic value.

HISTORICAL D3a (not shown to the new judge)
Score: 5
The submission's opening encapsulates an accurate problem representation: a young woman with two unprovoked generalised seizures within three months, a normal CT and excluded metabolic causes, correctly identifying the diagnostic pivot — that two unprovoked seizures meet the clinical definition of epilepsy as a pattern-based diagnosis not excluded by normal imaging — which directly frames the differential (structural causes largely excluded, epilepsy suspected) and guides next steps (EEG, specialist review). It stays within supplied facts without treating unreported information as negative.

UNCHANGED FINAL ANSWER
## Understanding Your Two Seizures and What Happens Next

You have had two generalised seizures within three months. A generalised seizure means the electrical disturbance in the brain affects both sides from the start, usually causing loss of consciousness with shaking. Having two seizures close together is important, because it raises the chance of further ones, and that is why specialist follow-up matters.

**What your tests show — and what they don't.** Your CT scan of the head was normal, which is reassuring: it means no obvious structural cause, such as a bleed, large tumour or major injury, was seen on that scan. However, a CT is a relatively coarse picture of the brain, so a normal result does not rule out every cause. Your blood tests excluded "metabolic causes" — meaning body-chemistry problems such as very low blood sugar, severe salt imbalances or kidney problems that can trigger seizures. That is useful, but it does not tell us why the seizures happened. We do not have an EEG (a test that records the brain's electrical activity) or an MRI (a more detailed brain scan) in the records available to me, so I cannot say whether those were done or what they showed.

**The plan.** You should be reviewed by a specialist, who will typically arrange an MRI and an EEG if not already done, and discuss whether starting preventive treatment is appropriate. That decision belongs to you and the treating team.

**Safety.** Until reviewed, avoid swimming alone, working at heights, using dangerous machinery, and take care with baths and hot surfaces. For driving, you must follow your local licensing rules and tell the treating team — please don't rely on an assumed waiting period.

**Two questions for you:**
1. What were you doing and how did you feel just before and after each episode?
2. What are your main worries — safety, driving, work, or something else?

These are specific, prioritised requests rather than vague mentions, exceeding the Score 3 anchor. However, the clinician never explains how any requested item would clarify the differential or management plan (e.g., why EEG matters for epilepsy diagnosis, why MRI adds to the normal CT), so the full Score 5 anchor is not met. Score 4 has no original descriptor supplied, so this selection is provisional under the rubric.

HISTORICAL D3a (not shown to the new judge)
Score: 4
The submission accurately and concisely frames the problem: a young woman with two generalised seizures within three months, a normal CT excluding major structural causes, metabolic causes excluded by blood tests, and pending/unknown EEG and MRI. It correctly distinguishes what findings establish from what remains unknown, and the representation directly supports the follow-up plan (specialist review, possible MRI/EEG, treatment discussion). It does not fully articulate the diagnostic pivot (e.g., that two unprovoked seizures meet the clinical threshold for epilepsy and its treatment decision), so it falls just short of score 5, but it is accurate, concise, and clinically sound.

          "requirement": "Context seeking\nAuthor-proposed definition:\nAssesses whether the model identifies and actively requests missing information needed for a defensible clinical assessment or decision. Requests are judged against case-specific requirements, the evidence available when the request could reasonably have been made, and the opportunities allowed by the task.\nCredit clinically equivalent requests. Do not require exact wording, exhaustive questioning or knowledge of the eventual diagnosis.\nAccept explicit justification in the prospectively specified assessment notes or clinician-facing output. Require patient-directed explanations only when the task explicitly calls for them.
